Scalar anomalous dissipation and optimal regularity via iterated homogenization Thanks: LSz gratefully acknowledges the support of the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) through GZ SZ 325/2-1.
Jan Burczak, László Székelyhidi, Jr, Bian Wu

Abstract
. For any β 0 < 1 / 3 \beta_{0}<1/3 we construct divergence free vector fields in C x , t β 0 C_{x,t}^{\beta_{0}} and a sequence of diffusivities κ q ↘ 0 \kappa_{q}\searrow 0 such that, for an arbitrary initial datum from a low regularity class, the classical solution ρ q \rho_{q} to the advection-diffusion equation exhibits anomalous dissipation along the sequence κ q \kappa_{q} . At the same time ρ q \rho_{q} remains uniformly bounded in C t 0 ​ C x α 0 C_{t}^{0}C_{x}^{\alpha_{0}} , where β 0 + 2 ​ α 0 < 1 \beta_{0}+2\alpha_{0}<1 . Our result confirms a conjecture of Armstrong and Vicol [ ArmstrongVicol ] and shows sharpness of the Obukhov-Corrsin threshold within the context of iterated homogenization. Our construction confirms time-homogeneity of the dissipation anomaly, as required in turbulence theory, and as a consequence we also obtain better time regularity for the scalar ρ q \rho_{q} than the classical prediction of Yaglom.



1.
Introduction

We consider the advection-diffusion equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{\kappa}+u\cdot\nabla\rho_{\kappa}=

\displaystyle\,\kappa\Delta\rho_{\kappa}

\displaystyle\text{ in }\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(0,1]\,,

(1.0.1)

\displaystyle\rho_{\kappa}(\cdot,0)=

\displaystyle\,\rho_{\mathrm{in}}

\displaystyle\text{ on }\mathbb{T}^{2},

where
u:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a given divergence-free vector field and
\rho_{\mathrm{in}}
is an initial datum with zero mean. Throughout this paper we only consider data
u,\rho_{\mathrm{in}}
which is regular enough for global well-posedness of (
1.0.1
) in the energy class to hold (e.g.
\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in L^{2}
and
u\in L^{\infty}L^{d}
,
d=2
, suffices). In this case the strong energy identity

\frac{1}{2}\|\rho_{\kappa}(T)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\rho_{\kappa}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,dt=\frac{1}{2}\|\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\quad\textrm{ for all }T\in[0,1]

(1.0.2)

holds. Furthermore, the solution to (
1.0.1
) is unique and as smooth as the datum allows. In what follows, we refer to such solutions as ’classical solution’ and note in passing that weaker notions of solution also exist (see e.g.
[
Modena2018
]
).

1.1.
Main result

In this paper we prove the following theorem:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
0<\beta_{0}<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
and
\alpha_{0}>0
such that
2\alpha_{0}+\beta_{0}<1
. There exists a divergence-free vector field

u\in C^{\beta_{0}}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1])

(1.1.1)

and a sequence of diffusivities
\{\kappa_{q}\}_{q\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\kappa_{q}\searrow 0
, such that for any zero-mean
\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
with
s>\frac{1+\beta_{0}}{2}
the classical solution
\rho_{q}:=\rho_{\kappa_{q}}
of (
1.0.1
) satisfies for any
T\in(0,1]

\lim_{q\rightarrow\infty}\kappa_{q}\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\rho_{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,dt\geq c_{0}\|\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,.

(1.1.2)

Moreover, if in addition
\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in H^{1+\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for some
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha_{0}
, then

\sup_{q\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\rho_{q}\|_{C^{\alpha_{0}}}\leq c_{1}\|\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\|_{H^{1+\alpha^{\prime}}}\,.

(1.1.3)

The constant
c_{0}>0
depends only on
u
,
T
and the ratio
\frac{\|\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\|_{2}}{\|\nabla^{s}\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\|_{2}}
, whereas the constant
c_{1}>0
depends only on
u
.

Theorem
1.1.1
is a substantial extension of the main result proved in
[
ArmstrongVicol
]
via iterated homogenization: the first statement above (
1.1.2
) is the subject of
[
ArmstrongVicol
, Theorem 1.1]
, but the second statement above (
1.1.3
) has merely been conjectured to hold in
[
ArmstrongVicol
, Section 1.3]
, and is in fact the main novelty of our work. We also remark that the first statement above in the 3D case has been substantially improved in our prior work
[
burczak2023anomalous
]
, where (i)
u
is in addition a weak solution of the 3D Euler equations satisfying an
h
-principle, and (ii) using the explicit dependence of
c_{0}
on the data and based on the fluctuation-dissipation relation introduced in
[
Drivas_Eyink_2017
]
, estimate (
1.1.2
) is complemented with a lower bound on the average rate of separation between two realizations of the associated stochastic differential equation of (
1.0.1
), in agreement with the Richardson law of dispersion.

1.2.
Context

In hydrodynamic turbulence theory, the advection-diffusion equation
(
1.0.1
) serves as an important toy model for ’turbulent transport’. For example, the classical K41 theory of turbulence based on the 3D Navier-Stokes equations finds its analogy in the Corrsin-Obukhov-Yaglom theory for the advection-diffusion equation. There are two foundational postulates common to both of these theories
[
Frisch_1995
, Section 6]
1
1
1
Here we focus on the possible mathematical implications of these postulates, notwithstanding well-founded doubts as to their validity in a physical context, such as scaling corrections due to intermittency or the recent work
[
iyer2025zerothlawturbulence
]
challenging the zeroth law.
:

(I)

finite non-vanishing mean rate of dissipation in the inviscid limit;

(II)

homogeneity and isotropy on small scales.

In the context of advection-diffusion (
1.0.1
) postulate (I) translates, in its most basic form, into the statement that

\inf_{\kappa>0}\kappa\int_{0}^{1}\|\nabla\rho_{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,dt\geq c_{0}

(1.2.1)

for some constant
c_{0}>0
, and is often referred to as the ’zeroth law of scalar turbulence’ or ’anomalous dissipation’. Examples of divergence-free vector fields
u
for which one can verify (
1.2.1
) have been constructed in
[
DriElgIyeJeo22
]
and shortly after in
[
CCS
,
ElgLis23
]
with optimal regularity properties (see Section
1.2.1
below for a more in-depth discussion). The drawback of these constructions is that they do not satisfy postulate (II). More precisely, the time-translation invariance of the underlying model in connection with postulate (II) requires, at the least, the strengthening of (
1.2.1
) to

\inf_{\kappa>0}\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\rho_{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,dt\geq c_{0}(T)\quad\forall T>0,

(1.2.2)

where
c_{0}(T)>0
. The works
[
ArmstrongVicol
,
burczak2023anomalous
]
as well as present work almost achieve (
1.2.2
), the only downside being that the infimum
\inf_{\kappa>0}
has to be replaced by
\limsup_{\kappa\to 0}
(indeed, it seems very difficult if not impossible to upgrade the
\limsup
to
\inf
within the iterated homogenization framework used here).

1.2.1.
Optimal regularity

The central prediction of K41 theory for Navier-Stokes turbulence, based on the hypothesis of anomalous dissipation, is a precise scaling law for the energy spectrum in the inertial range:
E(k)\sim c_{0}^{2/3}k^{-5/3}
. A mathematical formulation of this prediction has famously been put forward by L. Onsager in 1949, which became known as Onsager’s conjecture and is concerned with ’ideal turbulence’: the threshold regularity for conservation of energy in the Euler equations is precisely
C^{1/3}
on the Hölder scale (corresponding to monofractal scaling). We refer to
[
Eyink_survey
]
for an excellent recent survey on Onsager’s ideal turbulence and the current state of the art. The corresponding prediction in the context of passive scalar advection-diffusion is the Obukhov-Corrsin law and can be stated in the most basic form as follows
[
Obu49
,
Cor51
]
, see also
[
DriElgIyeJeo22
]
: if the advecting velocity field
u\in C^{\beta}
is Hölder continuous with exponent
\alpha>0
, then the solution
\rho_{\kappa}
of (
1.0.1
) should be
\rho_{\kappa}\in C^{\alpha}
uniformly in
\kappa>0
, with
\beta+2\alpha=1
, and not better. A more precise form of this prediction was obtained by Yaglom
[
Yaglom1949
]
, see also
[
CCS
]
, stating that the criticality threshold for anomalous dissipation is

u\in L_{t}^{p}C_{x}^{\beta},\;\rho_{\kappa}\in L_{t}^{r}C_{x}^{\alpha},\quad\text{uniformly in }\kappa,\qquad\frac{1}{p}+\frac{2}{r}=1,\;\beta+2\alpha=1.

(1.2.3)

The regular side of the Yaglom relation, namely the statement that no anomalous dissipation is possible provided

u\in L_{t}^{p}C_{x}^{\beta},\rho_{\kappa}\in L_{t}^{r}C_{x}^{\alpha},\quad\text{uniformly in }\kappa,\qquad\frac{1}{p}+\frac{2}{r}\leq 1,\;\beta+2\alpha>1

(1.2.4)

is known to hold, see
[
Eyi96
,
ConPro93
,
ConPro94
,
CCS
,
DriElgIyeJeo22
]
, with interesting connections to non-classical transport theory. Conversely, sharpness of the Yaglom regime (
1.2.4
) has been obtained in
[
CCS
]
by Colombo, Crippa and Sorella. More precisely, in
[
CCS
]
the authors prove that whenever
\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}+\nicefrac{{2}}{{r}}=1
and
\beta+2\alpha<1
with
2\leq r\leq 4
, there exists initial data
\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in C^{\infty}

and divergence-free
u\in L_{t}^{p}C_{x}^{\beta}
such that the classical solutions of (
1.0.1
) satisfy (
1.2.1
) and

\displaystyle\limsup_{\kappa\to 0}\|\rho_{\kappa}\|_{L^{r}_{t}C^{\alpha}_{x}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1])}\leq C_{0}.

(1.2.5)

This result of
[
CCS
]
has been extended by Elgindi and Liss in
[
ElgLis23
]
(based on ideas in
[
DriElgIyeJeo22
]
) at the endpoint time case
r=2
,
p=\infty
(in fact with velocity even continuous in time) by allowing for arbitrary smooth initial data.

Compared to these works, the novelty of our contribution is twofold: firstly, our estimates (
1.1.3
), (
1.1.1
) not only saturate the Obukhov-Corrsin relation from below, but in fact yield better time regularity. More importantly, we are able to achieve this in combination with the stronger, time-homogeneous form of anomalous dissipation (
1.1.2
). In this respect the work of Armstrong and Vicol was a major step forward in the field as it not only achieved (
1.1.2
) in agreement with postulate (II), but also identified iterated homogenization as the correct mechanism behind the phenomenon of anomalous dissipation (in agreement with the classical idea of eddy diffusivity). However, the authors of
[
ArmstrongVicol
]
left open the question of uniform regularity of the density, and conjectured that with their approach, combined with “large-scale regularity” techniques developed in quantitative homogenization theory, one can reach
C^{0}_{t}C^{\frac{1-\beta}{2}}_{x}
regularity of
\rho_{q}
uniformly in
q
(in agreement with the Obukhov-Corrsin prediction).

In this paper we confirm this conjecture. Our methods complement the ideas introduced in
[
ArmstrongVicol
]
, but we do not follow the ’large scale regularity’ approach suggested there. Instead, we develop a novel framework for iterated quantitative homogenization for high-order correctors.

1.2.2.
Outlook

We believe that this paper does not only contain a new result in the fine analysis of the advection-diffusion equation and in anomalous dissipation of scalar variance, but provides a set of useful and sharp technical tools. In particular, we believe these tools can allow to gain insights into the following themes, which we plan to address in a subsequent work:

(P.1)

Spatial homogeneity of anomalous dissipation.

We expect the anomalous dissipation constructed in Theorem
1.1
to be homogeneous both in space and in time, which would amount to complete justification of postulate (II). In technical terms, we expect that for any nonempty open parabolic cylinder
E\subset\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]
and generic initial datum,

\displaystyle\liminf_{q\rightarrow\infty}\kappa_{q}\iint_{E}|\nabla\rho_{q}|^{2}dxdt\geq c(E,\rho_{\mathrm{in}},u).

(1.2.6)

A further interesting question is then to characterize the dependence of
c(E,\rho_{\mathrm{in}},u)
on
E
,
\rho_{\mathrm{in}}
and
u
. We expect that the techniques in this paper will help such characterization.

(P.2)

H-principle and Euler equations.
Based on the work
[
burczak2023anomalous
]
bridging iterated homogenization in
[
ArmstrongVicol
]
and convex integration in
[
BDSV
]
, we expect the existence of a large class of turbulent vector fields
u
, exhibiting both homogeneous anomalous dissipation (in the sense of
(P.1)
) and optimal Hölder regularity for scalar fields, which are dense in an appropriate sense. This would yield an h-principle for these vector fields. Moreover, we expect they can be constructed as Hölder continuous weak solutions to the incompressible Euler equations, given the insights from convex integration
[
DSz13
,
Isett2018
,
BDSV
,
GiriRadu
]
.

1.2.3.
Related works

In the recent past there has been a surge of activity concerning the mathematical foundation of the phenomenon of anomalous dissipation and the closely related issue of mixing. Although a rigorous approach for the nonlinear Navier-Stokes case is still widely open, the case of the advection-diffusion equation (
1.0.1
) has seen several very interesting advances. Beyond the works already mentioned we wish to point out
[
CZDelElg20
,
ElgLisMat23
,
Yao2017
,
HCRR
,
HuyTit
,
JohSor
,
DRDII
,
TaoZwo
,
Ziz
]
. Finally, we mention the excellent surveys
[
Drivas_survey
,
Eyink_survey
]
for a wider overview and context of the subject.

1.3.
Challenges and main ideas

Our construction of the vector field
u
in Theorem
1.1
essentially follows the approach of
[
ArmstrongVicol
,
burczak2023anomalous
]
, arising iteratively from a sequence
\{u_{q}\}
, where
u_{q+1}=u_{q}+w_{q+1}
and
w_{q+1}
is a highly oscillatory perturbation with spatial frequency
\lambda_{q+1}
and temporal frequency
\mu_{q+1}
. The perturbation
w_{q+1}
is transported by
u_{q}
locally in time intervals of length
\mu_{q+1}^{-1}
- see for instance Section
2.3
. In a first approximation, we can think of such a perturbation as taking the form

\displaystyle w_{q+1}(x,t)=

\displaystyle\,a_{q}(x,t)\eta(\mu_{q+1}t)W\big(\lambda_{q+1}\Phi_{q}(x,t)\big).

(1.3.1)

In this way we obtain a sequence of advection-diffusion equations, with advecting vector field
u_{q}
and diffusion coefficient
\kappa_{q}\sim\lambda_{q}^{-\theta}
with
\theta\sim 1+\beta
, such that at each level
q
the deformation due to transport and the diffusion are balanced. In oversimplified terms the corresponding solutions
\rho_{q}
arise then from iteratively homogenizing equation
q+1
to equation
q
, see
[
burczak2023anomalous
, Section 2]
. Looking at the explicit form of the first order corrector and basic energy estimates in
[
ArmstrongVicol
,
burczak2023anomalous
]
suggests then the following naive approach to regularity: obtain inductive information on higher regularity of
\rho_{q+1}-\rho_{q}
in the form of Campanato bounds, and then to apply the tools developed for the ”large scale regularity” theory in stochastic homogenization
[
ArmstrongBook
]
. However, this strategy seems to run into two fundamental difficulties:

•

on small scales
\sim\lambda_{q}^{-1}
we have to deal with a lack of sufficiently large scale separation: The scale of separation is at most
\nicefrac{{\lambda_{q}}}{{\lambda_{q+1}}}
, which is very mild compared to the degeneracy in ellipticity
\kappa_{q+1}
. In order to achieve the optimal Hölder regularity, we thus need to compute correctors in the homogenization procedure to a certain very high order
Q
.

•

in contrast, on larger scales we see that the background transport
u_{q}
remains at order
1
, while the ellipticity coefficient
\kappa_{q}
degenerates to zero. As well shall see later, this ”high contrast environment” means that energy estimates and Grönwall or Schauder estimates are far from being sufficient for pursuing any useful uniform Hölder regularity of
\rho_{q}
.

Although the first issue is possibly more technical than fundamental, since high order expansions are by now well known in periodic homogenization and also in quasi-periodic or stochastic settings
[
Gu2017high
]
, the second issue seems to be a fundamental feature of our setup, prohibiting the use of ”large-scale regularity” techniques
[
AvLin1987
,
AvLin1989
]
. Instead, we are forced to develop a version of iterated homogenization which includes high order expansions. In turn this leads to new challenges, which are of a different nature from those encountered in quantitative stochastic homogenization
[
GloriaOtto
,
FischerOtto
,
ArmstrongBook
,
Armstrong2021homogenization
,
Armstrong2023Large
]
.

1.4.
Main ingredients of our approach

To prove Theorem
1.1
, we establish a regularity theory for these turbulent vector fields via higher order correctors. Below, we summarize the main new ingredients in this work.

1.4.1.
Expanding the flow maps to reveal higher order periodicity in time

Even though, as explained above, our velocity fluctuations
w_{q+1}
are heuristically expected to be periodic in space and time as in (
1.3.1
), this is only true upto first order. In fact, the nature of temporal cutoffs and the use of local-in-time flow maps means that the actual fluctuations are not periodic, neither in space nor in time. For the purposes of
[
ArmstrongVicol
,
burczak2023anomalous
]
this is sufficient, as effects from lower order terms have a negligible contribution in the energy norm
L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
. However, for the higher order corrector expansion these terms do become significant and they may lead to non-negligible contributions.

To recover periodicity in space and time, we define a globally defined approximate flow map
\Phi_{q}
arising from a temporal Taylor expansion of the local flow maps

\displaystyle\Phi_{q,\iota}=x+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}(t_{q,\iota}-t)^{m}D_{q,t}^{m-1}u_{q},

(1.4.1)

where
D_{q,t}:=\partial_{t}+u_{q}\cdot\nabla
is the transport derivative associated to
u_{q}
and
t_{q,\iota}
is the center of the support of
\eta_{\iota}
. Truncating this expansion at order
m_{*}
, we can retrieve

\displaystyle w_{q+1}(x,t)=

\displaystyle\,a_{q}(x,t)\sum_{m=0}^{m_{*}}\eta^{(m)}(\mu_{q+1}t)w\big(\lambda_{q+1}\Phi_{q}(x,t)\big)+O(\lambda_{q+1}^{-m_{*}\gamma_{0}})

(1.4.2)

for some small positive exponent
\gamma_{0}>0
. The flow map
\Phi_{q}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
is globally defined in time and
\eta^{(m)}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are globally periodic.

In (
1.4.2
),
a_{q}
contains large spatial scale
(\geq\lambda_{q}^{-1})
and large temporal scale
(\geq\mu_{q}^{-1})
,
\eta^{(m)}(\mu_{q+1}t)
contains fast temporal scale
\mu_{q+1}^{-1}
and
w(\lambda_{q+1}\Phi_{q})
contains fast spatial scale
\lambda_{q+1}^{-1}
. The expansion above reduces our problem, at each iteration step, to a cleaner homogenization problem up to arbitrarily small perturbations, at least formally, which is very desirable.

1.4.2.
Single space-time hybrid homogenization problem

With above expansion on flow maps, at each iteration step, we can focus on the following over-simplified advection diffusion equation in
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]
,

\begin{split}D_{q,t}\rho_{q+1}-\operatorname{div}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}\kappa_{q+1},&\,\psi\big(\lambda_{q+1}\Phi_{q}(x,t),\mu_{q+1}t\big)\\
-\psi\big(\lambda_{q+1}\Phi_{q}(x,t),\mu_{q+1}t\big),&\,\kappa_{q+1}\end{bmatrix}\nabla\rho_{q+1}\bigg)=&\,0,\\
\rho_{q+1}(\cdot,0)=&\,\rho_{\mathrm{in}}.\end{split}

(1.4.3)

From classical homogenization theory
[
BenLioPap78
,
JiKoOl
,
ShenBook
]
, we know there are three types of correctors that we have to consider. The first type is the spatial corrector, which captures the effect of fast spatial oscillation
\lambda_{q+1}^{-1}
. The second type is the temporal corrector, which captures the effect of temporal oscillation
\mu_{q+1}^{-1}
. The third type is the boundary corrector, which is needed to enforce initial condition on parabolic boundary
\{t=0\}
.

Obtaining a high order expansion for such equations seems highly delicate, because now we have 2 distinct small scales
\lambda_{q+1}^{-1}
and
\mu_{q+1}^{-1}
in addition to the large scale, with no apriori well-defined ordering among different powers. In other words, there is not a unqiue way to even compute a formal asymptotic expansion. We present an algorithmic way
to simultaneously compute all three types of correctors up to arbitrarily high order.
The details are presented in Section
6.5
and Section
6.6
.

1.4.3.
Chain of homogenization problems, renormalized differential operators and polynomial-derivative space

As mentioned above, at each step
q
we require an expansion of
\rho_{q+1}
up to very high order
Q
, forcing us to solve
Q
different homogenization problems at step
q
. But then this means we need to solve
Q^{2}
homogenization problems at step
q-1
. When
q
goes to infinity, this would require us to solve infinitely many homogenization problems at step
1
, which is not feasible.

To overcome this issue, we solve a chain of
Q^{2}
problems at step
q+1
. We solve the first
Q
problems by expansion techniques in Section
1.4.2
and the remaining
Q^{2}-Q
problems by the energy method. This is possible because of a special structure of the chains, consisting of two parts: the divergence form of the
Q
homogenized problems and a special algebraic structure of function coefficients, introduced by the deformation of the flow maps
\nabla\Phi_{q}
. In particular, the function coefficients in the homogenized problems are polynomials of
\nabla\Phi_{q}
and its derivatives. To efficiently implement estimates and capture their algebraic structure, we use renormalized differential operators and introduce a special polynomial-derivative space. The purpose is to be able to efficiently propagate both their algebraic structure and the estimates on chains of homogenization problems when passing from step
q+1
to step
q
. These tools are developed in Section
2.4
, Section
3
and Section
4
. The homogenization schemes are then presented in Section
6
and
7
.

1.4.4.
Inertial range, dissipative range and scale-by-scale decomposition of initial datum

In Section
1.4.1
, Section
1.4.2
and Section
1.4.3
, we have described the main new ingredients in a single homogenization step. Next, we briefly describe how to implement these iteratively in
q
and how to deal with arbitrary initial datum in
H^{1+\alpha^{\prime}}
containing small scales. There are two key differences with respect to
[
ArmstrongVicol
,
burczak2023anomalous
]
.

First, instead of viewing the initial datum containing large scales as a single entity, we decompose the initial datum into infinitely many energy shells a’la Littlewood-Paley adapted to the scales
\{\lambda_{q}\}_{q}
. For a single shell component, we inductively apply the homogenization expansion to resolve its energy spectrum.

Secondly, we do homogenization separately in the inertial range and the dissipative range. The inertial range is where the diffusivity is significantly enhanced, amounting to a large eddy effect. The dissipative range contains scales below the Kolmogorov scale. This approach is required largely due to the challenges mentioned in Section
1.3
.

1.5.
Notations

We use usual Einstein summation convention.

1.5.1.
Multi-index

We use
\mathcal{I}
to denote the set of multi-indices consisting of indices
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
and
t
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\{1,2,t\}^{n}.

(1.5.1)

If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}
, then there is a unique
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
with
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{1,2,t\}^{n_{0}}
. We use
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(i)
to denote
i
-th element in
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, then

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}(1),\boldsymbol{\alpha}(2),\ldots,\boldsymbol{\alpha}(n_{0})).

(1.5.2)

We use
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}
to denote the cardinality of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, and
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}
to denote the cardinality of
t
. Moreover, we define

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|:=

\displaystyle\,|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}+|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t},\quad[\boldsymbol{\alpha}]:=|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}+2|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{x}=

\displaystyle\,\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}\mid|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}=0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(i,j)=

\displaystyle\,\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}\mid|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}\geq i,|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}\geq j\},

and a special collection of differentiation indices via

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{*}=\big\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha}(|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|)\in\{1,2\}\big\}.\end{split}

(1.5.3)

Here,
\mathcal{I}(i,j)
consists of indices with at least
i

x
-elements and
j

t
-elements.

1.5.2.
Differentiation and transport derivative

We use
\partial^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
or
\nabla^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
to denote the usual partial derivative with index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
which only contains spatial derivatives. For a smooth vector
v:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]
, we define the transport derivative associated to
v
to be
D_{t}:=\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla
. Using
D_{1}:=\partial_{1}
and
D_{2}:=\partial_{2}
, we define, for any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
with
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|=n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}(1)}D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}(2)}\ldots D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}(n)}.

(1.5.4)

We call
D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
the generalized derivative associated to vector field
v
with index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.

Remark 1.2
.

For differentiation indices in
\mathcal{I}_{*}
defined in (
1.5.3
), the last element is
x
-element, hence the first derivative being applied is always space derivative.

In dimension
2
, we define
\nabla^{\perp}:=[\partial_{2},-\partial_{1}]^{T}
. A related and important set of notations
renormalized differential operator
is introduced in Section
2.4
.
For a matrix-valued function
H:\mathbb{T}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
, we define
\operatorname{div}H:=\partial_{k}H_{jk}
. Then if
H
is antisymmetric, we have
\operatorname{div}H=\nabla^{\perp}H_{12}
.
For a function
\Phi:\mathbb{T}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we define
\nabla\Phi:\mathbb{T}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
by
[\nabla\Phi]_{ij}:=\partial_{j}\Phi_{i}
.
We also define

\displaystyle\operatorname{adj}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}d&-b\\
-c&a\end{bmatrix}.

(1.5.5)

As a consequence,
\operatorname{adj}\circ\operatorname{adj}
is an identity operator and

\displaystyle(Av)^{\perp}=(\operatorname{adj}^{T}A)v^{\perp},\quad\text{for any }A\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2},v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

(1.5.6)

1.5.3.
Oscillatory variables in homogenization

(\cdot)
and
[\cdot]
are normal brackets.
\xi
and
\tau
are periodic variables
\xi\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{T}
.
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\xi}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\tau}
are the integrals in
\xi
and
\tau
respectively.
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\xi,\tau}
is the integral in both
\xi
and
\tau
.

For periodic variable
\tau\in\mathbb{T}
, we define
\partial_{\tau}^{-1}
to be the inverse operation of differentiation, mapping a function with zero
\tau
-average to another function with zero
\tau
-average. The analogous definition applies to
\partial_{\xi_{1}}^{-1}
and
\partial_{\xi_{2}}^{-1}
. We also define
\Delta_{\xi}^{-1}
to be the inverse Laplacian, mapping a function with zero
\xi
-average to another function with zero
\xi
-average.

1.5.4.
Constants

We use
\lesssim
to denote
\leq
up to an absolute constant. For any integer
p
, we use
\lesssim_{p}
to denote
\leq
up to an absolute constant to power
p
, i.e.

\displaystyle\cdot\lesssim_{p}\cdot\qquad\text{means}\qquad\cdot\leq c^{p}\cdot

(1.5.7)

for some absolute constant
c
.

1.5.5.
Norms

For
1\leq p\leq+\infty
, we use
\|\cdot\|_{p}
to denote the respective
L^{p}
norm of the function domain. We also use the following simplified norm notation for function
\rho:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\|\rho\|:=\|\rho\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}L_{x}^{2}}

(1.5.8)

2.
The vector field

In this section we construct our drift and provide certain useful preliminaries.

2.1.
Absolute parameters

By
absolute parameters
, we mean parameters that merely depend on
\beta,\alpha_{0},\alpha^{\prime},s
in the statement of Theorem
1.1
. They are fixed at the beginning of the paper and remain unchanged throughout the paper.

For
\beta,\alpha_{0},\alpha^{\prime},s\in(0,1)
with

\displaystyle\beta<\frac{1}{3},\quad 2\alpha_{0}+\beta<1,\quad\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha_{0},\quad s>\frac{1+\beta}{2},

we fix
b
satisfying

\displaystyle b\in\Big(1,\frac{11}{10}\Big),\quad\alpha^{\prime}\geq b(\alpha_{0}+1)-1,\quad s\geq\frac{b(1+\beta)}{(b+1)(2-b)},

(2.1.1)

and

\displaystyle 2\alpha_{0}+\beta+\frac{1+\beta}{b+1}(b-1)\alpha_{0}\leq 1.

(2.1.2)

We also fix a large enough constant
\lambda_{0}
and define the following parameters:

\displaystyle\lambda_{q}:=\big\lceil\lambda_{0}^{(b^{q})}\big\rceil,\quad\mu_{q}:=\lambda_{q}^{e},\quad\delta_{q}:=\lambda_{q}^{-2\beta},\quad\kappa_{q}:=\lambda_{q}^{-\theta},

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{2b}{b+1}(1+\beta),

(2.1.4)

then

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\kappa_{q}\lambda_{q}}=\lambda_{q}^{\frac{1+\beta}{b+1}(b-1)}.

(2.1.5)

We also fix several relatively small exponents (absolute constants):
\gamma_{I},\gamma_{R}>0
for renormalized differentiation and interpolation,
\gamma_{S}
for auxiliary scale control in homogenization,
\gamma>0
for the finest scale gap in homogenization. They satisfy the following relation

\displaystyle 4(\gamma_{I}+\gamma_{R})\leq\gamma_{S}\leq\frac{b-1}{b+1},\quad 3b\gamma\leq\min\{\gamma_{I},\gamma_{R}\}.

(2.1.6)

Then we define

\displaystyle\mathring{\lambda}_{q}:=\lambda_{q}^{1+b\gamma_{I}}\Bigg(\frac{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\kappa_{q}\lambda_{q}}\Bigg)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\quad\mathring{\mu}_{q}:=\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2b\gamma_{I}},\quad\varepsilon_{q}:=\frac{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2b\gamma_{R}}}{\mu_{q+1}}.

(2.1.7)

This leads to the following relation

\displaystyle\mathring{\mu}_{q}\lesssim\kappa_{q}\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}\lesssim\mathring{\mu}_{q}.

(2.1.8)

We require the following relations:

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathring{\lambda}_{q}}{\kappa_{q+1}\lambda_{q+1}^{2}}\cdot\lambda_{q+1}^{2\gamma_{S}}\leq

\displaystyle\,1,

(2.1.9)

\displaystyle\max\Bigg\{\frac{\mu_{q+1}}{\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathring{\lambda}_{q}},\frac{\varepsilon_{q}\kappa_{q+1}\lambda_{q+1}^{2}}{\delta^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{q+1}\mathring{\lambda}_{q}},\frac{\mathring{\mu}_{q}}{\varepsilon_{q}\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathring{\lambda}_{q}},\frac{\kappa_{q+1}\lambda_{q+1}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{q}\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1}}\Bigg\}\leq

\displaystyle\,\lambda^{\gamma_{S}},

(2.1.10)

A sufficient condition for (
2.1.9
) is that

\displaystyle 3b\gamma_{S}\leq\frac{1-(2b+1)\beta}{2(b+1)}.

(2.1.11)

A sufficient condition for (
2.1.10
) is (
2.1.6
), (
2.1.11
) and

\displaystyle 1-b\beta+\frac{(1+\beta)(b-1)}{2(1+b)}-\frac{b\gamma_{S}}{2}\leq be\leq 1-b\beta+\frac{(1+\beta)(b-1)}{2(1+b)}+\frac{b\gamma_{S}}{2}.

(2.1.12)

We define
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle 2\alpha+\beta=1+2b\gamma_{I}-2b^{2}\gamma.

(2.1.13)

We also fix absolute integers
N_{*},N,Q\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle N_{*}\gamma\geq 3,\quad Q\geq N\geq N_{*}^{4}.

(2.1.14)

Remark 2.1
.

(
2.1.11
) leads to the constraint
\beta<\frac{1}{3}
. The constraints (
2.1.10
) and (
2.1.12
) are more at the technical level. The essential constraint for parameters is

\displaystyle\mathring{\mu}_{q+1}>\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1}>\kappa_{q+1}\lambda_{q+1}^{2}>\mu_{q+1}>\mathring{\mu}_{q}>\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q},

(2.1.15)

which also leads to the constraint
\beta<\frac{1}{3}
. From direct computations, one can verify above relation from our constraints on parameters.

Remark 2.2
.

We have the following relations on parameters. From (
2.1.6
), (
2.1.7
), (
2.1.11
) and (
2.1.12
), we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{q}<1.

From (
2.1.5
), (
2.1.6
) and
\beta<\frac{1}{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathring{\lambda}_{q+1}>\lambda_{q+1}>\mathring{\lambda}_{q}>\lambda_{q}.

From (
2.1.6
), (
2.1.7
) and (
2.1.12
), we have

\displaystyle\mathring{\mu}_{q+1}>\mu_{q+1}>\mathring{\mu}_{q}>\mu_{q}.

2.2.
Cutoffs

First, in the following lemma, we define some cutoff functions.

Lemma 2.3
.

There exist smooth periodic functions
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and non-periodic
\tilde{\eta}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying the following

(B.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{1}^{2}(\tau)d\tau\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sin^{2}(2\pi s)ds=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{2}^{2}(\tau)d\tau\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sin^{2}(2\pi s)ds=1.

(2.2.1)

(B.2)

\operatorname{supp}\eta_{1}\subset\bigcup_{\iota\in\mathbb{Z}}(\iota+\frac{1}{8},\iota+\frac{3}{8})
and
\operatorname{supp}\eta_{2}\subset\bigcup_{\iota\in\mathbb{Z}}(\iota+\frac{5}{8},\iota+\frac{7}{8})
and for any
p\geq 0

\displaystyle|\partial_{\tau}^{p}\eta_{1}|+|\partial_{\tau}^{p}\eta_{2}|\lesssim p^{3p}

(2.2.2)

(B.3)

\tilde{\eta}(\tau)=1
for
\tau\in(-\frac{3}{8},\frac{3}{8})
and
\operatorname{supp}\tilde{\eta}\subset[-\frac{5}{8},\frac{5}{8}]
and for any
p\geq 0

\displaystyle|\partial_{\tau}^{p}\tilde{\eta}|\lesssim p^{3p}

(2.2.3)

(B.4)

Define

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}_{\iota}(\tau):=\tilde{\eta}(\tau-\iota),

(2.2.4)

then
\{\tilde{\eta}_{\iota}\}_{\iota\in\mathbb{Z}}
forms a partition of unity on
\mathbb{R}
.

The constant in all estimates is absolute.

Proof of Lemma
2.3
.

For the standard (smooth, compactly supported) mollifier

\phi(\tau)=C\exp\left(\frac{1}{\tau^{2}-1}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{(-1,1)}

we have the following estimate

|\partial_{\tau}^{p}\phi(\tau)|\leq C\left(\frac{8}{e}p\right)^{2p}\leq c_{0}p^{3p},

(2.2.5)

where the former is a direct computation.
Shifting, scaling and periodising
\phi
, we obtain
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
with their estimates.

The partition of unity
\tilde{\eta}(\tau)
can be produced as follows. Take function
r(\tau)
whose graph is a isosceles trapezoid, equal to
1
on
(-\nicefrac{{3}}{{8}}-\varepsilon_{0},\nicefrac{{3}}{{8}}+\varepsilon_{0})
and vanishing outside
[-\nicefrac{{5}}{{8}}+\varepsilon_{0},\nicefrac{{5}}{{8}}-\varepsilon_{0}]
. Copies of this function, appropriately shifted, yield (non-smooth) partition of unity. Mollification of
r(\tau)
, by convolution with
\varepsilon^{-1}_{0}\phi(\frac{\cdot}{\varepsilon_{0}})
, produces function
\tilde{\eta}(\tau)
with bounds on derivatives inherited from the bound (
2.2.5
). Observe that, thanks to symmetry, shifted copies of
\tilde{\eta}(\tau)
remain a partition of unity (now smooth).
∎

2.3.
Construction of the vector field

In this section, we construct two divergence-free vector fields
\{u_{q}\}_{q}
and
\{v_{q}\}_{q}
by induction. The
\{\psi_{q}\}_{q}
are the stream functions of
\{u_{q}\}_{q}
. Then we define the limit vector field
u
and its stream function
\psi
via

\displaystyle u:=\lim_{q\rightarrow\infty}u_{q},\quad\psi:=\lim_{q\rightarrow\infty}\psi_{q}.

(2.3.1)

Define

\displaystyle u_{0}=v_{0}=0.

(2.3.2)

Assume we have
u_{q}
and
v_{q}
. Below we construct
u_{q+1}
and
v_{q+1}
.
At the induction step, define
\{t_{q,\iota}\}_{\iota\in\mathbb{Z}}
via
t_{q,\iota}:=\frac{\iota}{\mu_{q+1}}
and

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}_{q,\iota}(t):=\tilde{\eta}_{\iota}(\mu_{q+1}t).

(2.3.3)

Here,
\tilde{\eta}_{\iota}
is given in Lemma
2.3
. Then
\tilde{\eta}_{q,\iota}=1
on
(t_{q,\iota}-\frac{3}{8\mu_{q+1}},t_{q,\iota}+\frac{3}{8\mu_{q+1}})
and
\operatorname{supp}\tilde{\eta}_{q,\iota}\subset(t_{q,\iota}-\frac{5}{8\mu_{q+1}},t_{q,\iota}+\frac{5}{8\mu_{q+1}})
.

Next, we define two local flow maps
\bar{\Phi}_{q,\iota},\Phi_{q,\iota}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{2}
. Here,
\bar{\Phi}_{q,\iota}
solves the transport equation

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\bar{\Phi}_{q,\iota}+(u_{q}\cdot\nabla)\bar{\Phi}_{q,\iota}=&\,0,\\
\bar{\Phi}_{q,\iota}(x,t_{q,\iota})=&\,x.\end{split}

(2.3.4)

And
\Phi_{q,\iota}
is given by the following explicit formula

\displaystyle\Phi_{q,\iota}=x+\sum_{m=1}^{N_{*}}\frac{1}{m!}(t_{q,\iota}-t)^{m}D_{q,t}^{m-1}u_{q}.

(2.3.5)

We define the global flow maps
\bar{\Phi}_{q},\Phi_{q}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{2}
via

\displaystyle\bar{\Phi}_{q}=\sum_{\iota}\bar{\Phi}_{q,\iota}\tilde{\eta}_{q,\iota},\quad\Phi_{q}=\sum_{\iota}\Phi_{q,\iota}\tilde{\eta}_{q,\iota}.

(2.3.6)

We define
antisymmetric matrix
-valued function
H:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
via

\displaystyle H_{12}(\xi,\tau):=

\displaystyle\,\eta_{1}(\tau)\Pi_{1}(\xi)+\eta_{2}(\tau)\Pi_{2}(\xi)=-H_{21}(\xi,\tau),

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle\Pi_{1}(\xi)=

\displaystyle\,\sin(2\pi\xi_{1}),\quad\Pi_{2}(\xi)=\sin(2\pi\xi_{2}),

(2.3.8)

with
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
given by Lemma
2.3
. Then we define the stream functions

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{q+1}(x,t):=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{q+1}}H_{12}\big(\lambda_{q+1}\bar{\Phi}_{q}(x,t),\mu_{q+1}t\big),

(2.3.9)

\displaystyle\psi_{q+1}(x,t):=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{q+1}}\det\nabla\Phi_{q}H_{12}\big(\lambda_{q+1}\Phi_{q}(x,t),\mu_{q+1}t\big),

(2.3.10)

We construct
\{u_{q}\}_{q}
and
\{v_{q}\}_{q}
inductively via

\displaystyle u_{q+1}=

\displaystyle\,u_{q}+\bar{w}_{q+1},\quad\bar{w}_{q+1}=\nabla^{\perp}\bar{\psi}_{q+1}

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle v_{q+1}=

\displaystyle\,u_{q}+w_{q+1},\quad w_{q+1}=\nabla^{\perp}\psi_{q+1}.

(2.3.12)

Here,
v_{q+1}
is not iteratively defined by
v_{q}
. Instead,
v_{q+1}
is defined to be a perturbation of
u_{q+1}
.

Define

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{q+1}:=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{j=0}^{q+1}\bar{\psi}_{j},

(2.3.13)

\displaystyle\Psi_{q+1}:=

\displaystyle\,\bar{\Psi}_{q}+\psi_{q+1},

(2.3.14)

then we have

\displaystyle u_{q}

\displaystyle\,=\nabla^{\perp}\bar{\Psi}_{q},\quad v_{q}=\nabla^{\perp}\Psi_{q},

(2.3.15)

\displaystyle\bar{w}_{q+1}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{q+1}}\operatorname{div}\Big(H(\lambda_{q+1}\bar{\Phi}_{q},\mu_{q+1}t)\Big),

(2.3.16)

\displaystyle w_{q+1}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{q+1}}\operatorname{div}\Big(\det\nabla\Phi_{q}H(\lambda_{q+1}\Phi_{q},\mu_{q+1}t)\Big).

(2.3.17)

From the support information of the cutoff functions
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
in Lemma
2.3
and the construction of the velocity fields, we have the following property

\displaystyle\nabla^{p}u_{q}(x,0)=\nabla^{p}v_{q}(x,0)=0,\quad\forall\,p,q\in\mathbb{N},x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}.

(2.3.18)

We also define a support condition.

Definition 2.4

(Support condition)
.

A function
\eta:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
has support disjoint to
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
if

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\eta\cap\operatorname{supp}\eta_{1}=\operatorname{supp}\eta\cap\operatorname{supp}\eta_{2}=\varnothing.

(2.3.19)

Definition 2.5

(Shear condition)
.

A pair of functions
(\chi,\eta)
with
\chi:\mathbb{T}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\eta:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfy the shear condition if either

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\eta\subset\operatorname{supp}\eta_{1},\quad\partial_{\xi_{2}}\chi=0,\qquad\text{or}\qquad\operatorname{supp}\eta\subset\operatorname{supp}\eta_{2},\quad\partial_{\xi_{1}}\chi=0.

(2.3.20)

A pair of functions
(\chi,\eta)
satisfy the generalized shear condition if either it satisfies shear condition or

\displaystyle\nabla_{\xi}\chi=0.

(2.3.21)

2.4.
The polynomial-derivative space

We introduce now quantities useful for keeping track of forthcoming estimates of stream function, while high-order homogenisation is applied.

Recall the notations in Section
1.5.1
and Section
1.5.2
.

Definition 2.6

(Renormalized derivatives)
.

For any differentiation index
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\mathcal{I}
, let
D_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
be the
generalized derivative associated to
the vector field
u_{q}
with index
\boldsymbol{\omega}
. Define a
renormalized differential operator associated to

u_{q}
via

\displaystyle\mathring{D}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{-|\boldsymbol{\omega}|_{x}}\mathring{\mu}_{q}^{-|\boldsymbol{\omega}|_{t}}D_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}.

(2.4.1)

Similarly, we define
O_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
to be the
generalized derivative associated to
the vector field
v_{q}
with
\boldsymbol{\omega}
. We also define the
renormalized differential operator associated to

v_{q}
via

\displaystyle\mathring{O}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{-|\boldsymbol{\omega}|_{x}}\mathring{\mu}_{q}^{-|\boldsymbol{\omega}|_{t}}O_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}.

(2.4.2)

The same definition also extends to the space derivatives
\mathring{\nabla}_{q}
,
\mathring{\partial}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
and
\mathring{\operatorname{div}}_{q}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathring{\nabla}_{q}:=\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{-1}\nabla,\quad\mathring{\partial}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:=\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{-|\boldsymbol{\omega}|}\partial^{\boldsymbol{\omega}},\quad\mathring{\operatorname{div}}_{q}:=\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{-1}\operatorname{div}

(2.4.3)

for
\boldsymbol{\omega}
only containing space derivatives.

Note that, for fixed
q
, the renormalized
space
derivatives are the same for different vector fields
u_{q}
and
v_{q}
.

From the construction,
u_{q}
and
v_{q}
are smooth for any
q\in\mathbb{N}
. Now we define the following algebra generated by derivatives of their stream functions.

Definition 2.7

(Polynomial-derivative spaces
\mathcal{P}_{q}
and
\mathcal{O}_{q}
)
.

For any
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, recalling (
2.3.13
) and (
2.3.14
), we define

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0}):=\Bigg\{&\,a_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}a_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{I_{k}}\mathring{D}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}}\Bigg(\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\bar{\Psi}_{q}\Bigg)\,\Bigg|\,k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}.\,\{I_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq k_{0}}\subset\mathbb{N}^{+}.\\
&\,\{a_{k}\}_{0\leq k\leq k_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}.\,\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}\}_{1\leq k\leq k_{0},1\leq i\leq I_{k}}\subset\mathcal{I}(2,0)\cup\mathcal{I}(1,N_{*}),\sup_{k}\sum_{i}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}|\leq n_{0}\Bigg\}\end{split}

(2.4.4)

and

\begin{split}\mathcal{O}_{q}(n_{0}):=\Bigg\{&\,a_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}a_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{I_{k}}\mathring{O}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}}\Bigg(\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\Psi_{q}\Bigg)\,\Bigg|\,\\
&\,k_{0},I_{k},a_{k},\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}\text{ as above},\,\sup_{k}\sum_{i}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}|\leq n_{0}\Bigg\}.\end{split}

(2.4.5)

Remark 2.8
.

The prefactor
\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}
is chosen such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{q}\mu_{q}}\nabla u_{q}=\frac{1}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\nabla u_{q}=\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\mathring{\nabla}_{q}\mathring{\nabla}^{\perp}_{q}\bar{\Psi}_{q}\quad\rightarrow\quad{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{q}\mu_{q}}\nabla u_{q}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}\sim 1.

(2.4.6)

We also define a norm-like quantity for these polynomial-derivative spaces. Note that these are nowhere rigorously close to the notion of norm in Banach spaces.

Definition 2.9

(Norm-like quantity of polynomial-derivative space)
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
, we define a number
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}
via

\begin{split}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}:=\inf\Bigg\{\sum_{k=0}^{k_{0}}|a_{k}|\,\Bigg|\,f\text{ can be written }f=a_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}a_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{I_{k}}\mathring{D}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}}\Bigg(\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\bar{\Psi}_{q}\Bigg)&\,\\
\text{with }k_{0},I_{k},a_{k},\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i},n_{0}\text{ satisfying the same conditions in }\eqref{e:0:poly_Uq}&\,\Bigg\}\end{split}

(2.4.7)

Similarly, we also define the number
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}
for each element
f\in\mathcal{O}_{q}(n_{0})
.

The infimum in (
2.4.7
) is actually a minimum. This is explained in the following remark.

Remark 2.10
.

An element in
f\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
has multiple representations of form

\displaystyle f=a_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}a_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{I_{k}}\mathring{D}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}}\Bigg(\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\bar{\Psi}_{q}\Bigg),

(2.4.8)

since one can switch the order of differentiation in indices
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}
. However,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}
is uniquely defined for fixed
q
, because the infimum is taken among all representations. For fixed
n_{0}
, such representations live in a space with finite dimension, therefore the infimum in (
2.4.7
) is actually a minimum.

Definition 2.11

(Lower bound of differentiation order)
.

We say the differentiation order of
f\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
is bounded from below by
n\in\mathbb{N}
if there exists a representation (
2.4.8
) satisfying

\displaystyle\sup_{1\leq k\leq k_{0}}\sum_{i}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}|\leq n_{0},\quad\sum_{k=0}^{k_{0}}|a_{k}|={|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}

(2.4.9)

such that
a_{0}=0
and

\displaystyle\inf_{1\leq k\leq k_{0}}\sum_{i}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}|\geq n.

(2.4.10)

We also extend the same definition to
\mathcal{O}_{q}(n_{0})
.

Given above definitions, we have the following lemma on a truncation projection regarding the differentiation order.

Lemma 2.12

(Truncation on differentiation order)
.

For any
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and any element
f\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
and any integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a projection of
f
denoted by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}f\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n)

(2.4.11)

such that the differentiation order of
f-\mathbb{P}_{n}f\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
is bounded from below by
n
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbb{P}_{n}f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f-\mathbb{P}_{n}f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}={|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}.

(2.4.12)

The same result also holds for
\mathcal{O}_{q}(n_{0})
.

Proof.

We choose a representation of
f
like (
2.4.8
) satisfying (
2.4.9
), then we define

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}f:=

\displaystyle\,a_{0}+\sum_{k\in\blacksquare_{n}}a_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{I_{k}}\mathring{D}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}}\Bigg(\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\bar{\Psi}_{q}\Bigg),

\displaystyle\blacksquare_{n}:=

\displaystyle\,\bigg\{1\leq k\leq k_{0}\,\bigg|\,\sum_{i}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}|\leq n\bigg\}.

∎

Remark 2.13
.

Here, we do not claim the truncation projection in Lemma
2.12
is unique.

The elements in
\mathcal{O}_{q}
are approximations of elements in
\mathcal{P}_{q}
. Now we introduce the notion of congruence to formalize this correspondence.

Definition 2.14

(Congruence)
.

Fix
q\geq 1
. For some
g\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
and
h\in\mathcal{O}_{q}(n_{1})
, we say
g
and
h
are
congruent
if there exist
k_{0},\{I_{k}\}_{k},\{a_{k}\}_{k},\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}\}_{k,i}
as in (
2.4.4
), satisfying that

\displaystyle g=

\displaystyle\,a_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}a_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{I_{k}}\mathring{D}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}}\Bigg(\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\bar{\Psi}_{q}\Bigg),\quad h=a_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}a_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{I_{k}}\mathring{O}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}}\Bigg(\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\Psi_{q}\Bigg).

(2.4.13)

Remark 2.15
.

For
a\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(0)=\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|a|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}:=|a|.

(2.4.14)

The same applies to
\mathcal{O}_{q}(n_{0})
.

Remark 2.16
.

From the support of the cutoff function in Lemma
2.3
and the definition of
\bar{\Psi}_{q}
, any element
h
in
\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
satisfies

\displaystyle h(x,t)\text{ is constant }\forall\,(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\bigg[0,\frac{1}{8\mu_{q}}\bigg],\quad\text{ and }\quad|h(x,t)|\leq{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|h|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q},\,\forall\,(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\bigg[0,\frac{1}{8\mu_{q}}\bigg].

The same applies to
\mathcal{O}_{q}(n_{0})
.

Remark 2.17
.

For any
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
and
\mathcal{O}_{q}(n_{0})
are linear spaces.

Remark 2.18
.

If
f\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{1})
and
g\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle fg\in

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{1}+n_{2}),

(2.4.15)

\displaystyle f+g\in

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{P}_{q}(\max\{n_{1},n_{2}\}),

(2.4.16)

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f+g|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}\leq

\displaystyle\,{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|g|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q},

(2.4.17)

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|fg|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}\leq

\displaystyle\,{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|g|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q},

(2.4.18)

\displaystyle\nabla f,D_{q,t}f\in

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{1}+1).

(2.4.19)

The same applies to
\mathcal{O}_{q}
.

3.
Analysis of stream functions

In this section, we prove some estimates related to the stream functions
\{\bar{\Psi}_{q}\}_{q}
and
\{\Psi_{q}\}_{q}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Recall
N_{*},Q
and
N(N_{*})
fixed in (
2.1.14
).
Recall
H
defined in (
2.3.7
), the flow map
\Phi_{q}
defined in (
2.3.5
) (which gives number
N_{*}
), the flow map
\bar{\Phi}_{q}
defined in (
2.3.5
) (
2.3.6
) and the vector field
u_{q}
defined (
2.3.11
).

There exist smooth functions

\displaystyle B_{q,m},E_{q,m},S_{q,m},\Omega_{q,m}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2},

\displaystyle\omega_{q,m}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle\varpi_{q,m}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\phi_{m},\varphi_{m},z_{m},\vartheta_{m},\sigma_{m},\zeta_{m}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

indexed by
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq m\leq N(N_{*})
, such that:

(I) We have the following identities for any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]
,
\xi\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{T}
(below, if the argument on the left hand side is
(x,t)
, we omit it for brevity).

\displaystyle\nabla\Phi_{q}=

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon_{q}\sum_{m=1}^{N}B_{q,m}(x,t){\phi_{m}}(\mu_{q+1}t),

(3.0.1)

\displaystyle{\det\nabla\Phi_{q}}=

\displaystyle\,{1+\varepsilon_{q}}{\sum_{m=1}^{N}\varpi_{q,m}(x,t)\zeta_{m}(\mu_{q+1}t),}

(3.0.2)

\displaystyle\nabla\Phi_{q}\nabla\Phi_{q}^{T}-(\det\nabla\Phi_{q})^{2}\mathrm{Id}=

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon_{q}\sum_{m=1}^{N}S_{q,m}(x,t)\vartheta_{m}(\mu_{q+1}t),

(3.0.3)

\displaystyle\operatorname{adj}\nabla\Phi_{q}^{T}\det\nabla\Phi_{q}=

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon_{q}\sum_{m=1}^{N}E^{T}_{q,m}(x,t)\varphi_{m}(\mu_{q+1}t),

(3.0.4)

\displaystyle{\big((\det\nabla\Phi_{q})H(\xi,\tau)}\big)_{ij}=

\displaystyle\,H_{ij}(\xi,\tau)+\varepsilon_{q}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\sigma_{m}(\tau)\Omega_{q,m,ij}(x,t)H_{12}(\xi,\tau),

(3.0.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{\iota\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\eta}_{q,\iota}D_{q,t}\Phi_{q,\iota}=

\displaystyle\,{\omega_{q,N_{*}}}(x,t){\phi_{N_{*}}}(\mu_{q+1}t),

(3.0.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{\iota\in\mathbb{Z}}\partial_{t}\tilde{\eta}_{q,\iota}\Phi_{q,\iota}=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{m=1}^{{N_{*}}}{\omega_{q,m}}(x,t)z_{m}(\mu_{q+1}t),

(3.0.7)

with

\displaystyle\omega_{q,N}\in(\mathcal{P}_{q}(N))^{2},\quad\varpi_{q,m}\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(N),

(3.0.8)

\displaystyle B_{q,m},E_{q,m},S_{q,m},\Omega_{q,m}\in(\mathcal{P}_{q}(N))^{2\times 2}.

(3.0.9)

Furthermore, for any
1\leq m\leq N
,
z_{m}
has support disjoint to
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
.

(II) We have the following estimates.
For
p\lesssim 8Q^{3}
the functions defined in (I) satisfy

\|\partial_{\tau}^{p}\phi_{m}\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{\tau}^{p}\varphi_{m}\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{\tau}^{p}z_{m}\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{\tau}^{p}\vartheta_{m}\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{\tau}^{p}\sigma_{m}\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{\tau}^{p}\zeta_{m}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\,1.

(3.0.10)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{N}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|B_{q,m}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|E_{q,m}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|S_{q,m}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\Omega_{q,m}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\omega_{q,m}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\varpi_{q,m}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}\lesssim

\displaystyle 1.

(3.0.11)

\displaystyle{{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\omega_{q,{N_{*}}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\lambda_{q+1}^{-3},

(3.0.12)

The constant in
\lesssim
depends in fact only on
Q
(since by choice (
2.1.14
)
N\leq Q
and
N_{*}\leq Q
, and
Q
is an absolute constant).

Lemma
3.1
is proven in Section
3.1
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For the cumulative stream function
\bar{\Psi}_{q+1}
defined in (
2.3.13
), and for the stream function
\bar{\psi}_{q+1}
defined in (
2.3.9
), we have

\|\nabla^{n}\bar{\Psi}_{q+1}\|_{\infty}\leq\,C(n)\frac{\delta_{{q+1}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{{q+1}}}\lambda_{{q+1}}^{n}\qquad\text{for any }n\geq 2

(3.0.13)

and

\lVert\nabla^{n}\bar{\psi}_{{q+1}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C(n)\frac{\delta_{{q+1}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{{q+1}}}\lambda_{{q+1}}^{n}\qquad\text{for any }n\geq 0.

(3.0.14)

For any fixed number
Y
we have also

\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}\bar{\Psi}_{q+1}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\,1\vee\frac{\delta_{{q+1}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{{q+1}}}(\delta_{{q+1}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{{q+1}})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{{q+1}}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}\quad\text{for any }|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq Y

(3.0.15)

and

\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{{q+1}}\bar{\psi}_{q+1}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\frac{\delta_{{q+1}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{{q+1}}}(\delta_{{q+1}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{{q+1}})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{{q+1}}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}\qquad\text{for any }|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq Y.

(3.0.16)

The constant
C(n)=\frac{(n-1)!}{n^{2}}C^{n-1}\tilde{C}
, for certain absolute constants, and
\lesssim
depends only on the upper bound
Y
. The constants can be taken
C=50\pi
and
\tilde{C}=2
. Importantly, none of the constants depend on
q
.

Lemma
3.2
is proven in Section
3.2
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Take
Y
fixed in Lemma
3.2
. For the cumulative stream function
\Psi_{q+1}
defined in (
2.3.14
), and the stream functions
\bar{\psi}_{q+1}
,
\psi_{q+1}
defined in (
2.3.9
), (
2.3.10
), we have for any
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq Y-N_{*}-1

\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}\Psi_{q+1}\|_{\infty}+\|O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}\Psi_{q+1}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\,1\vee\frac{\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{q+1}}(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}

(3.0.17)

\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}\psi_{q+1}\|_{\infty}+\|O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}\psi_{q+1}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\frac{\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{q+1}}(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}.

(3.0.18)

For their difference
\bar{\psi}_{q+1}-\psi_{q+1}=\bar{\Psi}_{q+1}-\Psi_{q+1}
it holds, recalling
\gamma
of section
2.1
, for any
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq Y-N_{*}-1

\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}(\bar{\psi}_{q+1}-\psi_{q+1})\|_{\infty}+\|O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}(\bar{\psi}_{q+1}-\psi_{q+1})\|_{\infty}\lesssim\lambda^{-\gamma N_{*}+1}_{q+1}\frac{\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{q+1}}(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}.

(3.0.19)

For the difference of derivative operators it holds for any
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq Y-N_{*}-1

\|(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1})\bar{\psi}_{q+1}\|_{\infty}+\|(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1})\psi_{q+1}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\lambda^{-\gamma N_{*}+1}_{q+1}\delta_{q+1}(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}.

(3.0.20)

as well as

\|(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1})\bar{\Psi}_{q+1}\|_{\infty}+\|(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1})\Psi_{q+1}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\delta^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{q+1}\lambda^{-\gamma N_{*}+2}_{q+1}(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}.

(3.0.21)

The constant in
\lesssim
depends only on the upper bound
Y
.

Corollary 3.4
.

In fact, denoting by

\Theta^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}
either
D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}
or
O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}
, any sequence consisting of symbols
\Theta^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}_{q+1}
and
(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}}_{q+1})
acting on either
\bar{\psi}_{q+1}
or on
\psi_{q+1}
can be estimated as (
3.0.20
), and acting on either
\bar{\psi}_{q+1}
or on
\psi_{q+1}
can be estimated as (
3.0.21
), provided there is at least one term
(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}}_{q+1})
. More precisely, for
f
being either
\bar{\psi}_{q+1}
or
\psi_{q+1}
and for
F
being either
\bar{\Psi}_{q+1}
or
\Psi_{q+1}
we have, for any
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq Y-N_{*}-1

\|(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}}_{q+1})\Theta^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}}_{q+1}(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}}_{q+1})\dots\Theta^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}}_{q+1}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim\lambda^{-\gamma N_{*}+1}_{q+1}\delta_{q+1}(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}\text{ etc},

(3.0.22)

\|(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}}_{q+1})\Theta^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}}_{q+1}(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}}_{q+1})\dots\Theta^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}}_{q+1}F\|_{\infty}\lesssim\delta^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{q+1}\lambda^{-\gamma N_{*}+2}_{q+1}(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}\text{ etc},

(3.0.23)

where ’etc’ means that the innermost and outermost operator actiong on
f
may be either
\Theta^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}_{q+1}
or
(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}_{q+1})
(so there are three more cases for l.h.s. of (
3.0.22
)).

Corollary 3.5
.

Fix positive
M
. For any smooth function
f:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, satisfying

\emph{either}\quad\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim M(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}\qquad\text{for any }|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq Y-N_{*}-n_{0}.

(3.0.24)

\emph{or}\quad\|O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim M(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}\qquad\text{for any }|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq Y-N_{*}-n_{0}.

(3.0.25)

with any
n_{0}\geq 1
,
n_{0}\leq Y-N_{*}-1
, the analogues of estimates (
3.0.20
), (
3.0.22
) hold for any
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq Y-N_{*}-n_{0}
. More precisely, we have for any
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq Y-N_{*}-n_{0}

\|(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q+1})f\|_{\infty}\lesssim M\delta^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{q+1}\lambda^{-\gamma N_{*}+2}_{q+1}(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}.

(3.0.26)

\|(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}}_{q+1})\Theta^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}}_{q+1}(D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}}_{q+1}-O^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}}_{q+1})\dots\Theta^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}}_{q+1}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim M\delta^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{q+1}\lambda^{-\gamma N_{*}+2}_{q+1}(\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+1})^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{t}}\lambda_{q+1}^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{x}}\text{ etc}.

(3.0.27)

Lemma
3.3
and its Corollaries are proven in Section
3.3
.

Corollary 3.6
.

Recall the space
\mathcal{P}_{q}
defined in (
2.4.4
). For any
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, and any
f\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
, we have the following estimates for
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|+n_{0}\leq Y
,

\displaystyle\|\mathring{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}\lambda_{q}^{-|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\gamma_{R}}.

(3.0.28)

If the differentiation order of
f
is bounded from below by some
n_{*}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|\mathring{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}\lambda_{q}^{-(|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|+n_{*})\gamma_{R}}.

(3.0.29)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we consider
f\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
that is not constant, we consider the representation of
f
that attains
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q+1}
, i.e.

\displaystyle f=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}a_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{I_{k}}\mathring{D}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}}\Bigg(\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\bar{\Psi}_{q}\Bigg),\quad\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}|a_{k}|={|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|g|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}.

(3.0.30)

Applying Lemma
3.2
, we have

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\mathring{D}_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}}\Bigg(\frac{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}^{2}}{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+2\gamma_{R}}}\bar{\Psi}_{q}\Bigg)\Bigg\|_{\infty}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\lambda_{q}^{-2\gamma_{R}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{q}}{\mathring{\lambda}_{q}}\bigg)^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}|_{x}-2}\bigg(\frac{\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}}{\mathring{\mu}_{q}}\bigg)^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}|_{t}}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{-|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,i}|\gamma_{R}}.

(3.0.31)

Here we use (
2.1.5
), (
2.1.6
) and (
2.1.7
).

Now (
3.0.28
) follows from (
3.0.31
). Recalling Definition
2.11
, (
3.0.29
) follows similarly.
∎

Corollary 3.7
.

Recall the spaces
\mathcal{O}_{q}
defined in (
2.4.5
). For any
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, and any
f\in\mathcal{O}_{q}(n_{0})
, we have the following estimates for
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|+n_{0}\leq Y
,

\displaystyle\|\mathring{O}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}\lambda_{q}^{-|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\gamma_{R}}.

(3.0.32)

If the differentiation order of
f
is bounded from below by some
n_{*}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|\mathring{O}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{q}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{q}\lambda_{q}^{-(|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|+n_{*})\gamma_{R}}.

(3.0.33)

Corollary 3.8
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{O}_{q}(n_{0})
and
g\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(n_{0})
, suppose
f
and
g
are congruent. Then we have the following estimates for any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}
with
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|+n_{0}\leq Y-N_{*}
,

Remark 3.9

(

vs

)
.

Observe that (

, whereas (

etc, and in order not to keep track of the precise value of the largest index in final formulas, we choose the upper bound for all of them, i.e.

Remark 3.10

(Multiple indices into long sums)
.

We will order terms with two (and more) indices into terms with single index according to the following version of enumerating product of two (and more) countable sets: having element
a_{m,m^{\prime}}
we define
b_{j}
as follows:

b_{j}=\begin{cases}a_{l,l}&\text{ if }j=l^{2}\\
a_{i,l}&\text{ if }j=(l-1)^{2}+i,\;i=1,\dots,l-1\\
a_{l,i}&\text{ if }j=(l-1)^{2}+i,\;i=l,\dots,2l-1\end{cases}

Importantly for precise control of constants in estimates

\displaystyle(i)

this enumeration does not involve
N_{*}

(3.1.3)

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle m,m^{\prime}\leq j\text{ i.e. any of the double indices is not larger than the single index.}

The full extent of the constants control is not necessary in the current paper.

In particular, define

|\partial_{\tau}^{p}(fg)|\leq 2^{p}|\sup_{i\leq p}\partial_{\tau}^{i}f||\sup_{j\leq p}\partial_{\tau}^{j}g|.

(3.1.16)

3.1.16

\phi_{m,\iota}(\tau)=\frac{1}{m!}(\iota-\tau)^{m}\tilde{\eta}(\tau-\iota),

|\iota-\tau|\leq 1

|\partial_{\tau}^{p}\phi_{m,\iota}|\leq 2^{p}c_{0}p^{3p}

and observing that in definition of
\phi_{m}(\tau)
at most two

’s overlap, we have

where

follows by the assumed absolute bound on total amount of derivatives,

.
The estimate (
3.1.17
) is the part of (

) where

is estimated.
Analogously, via the definition (

is estimated.
The product

\displaystyle|\partial_{\tau}^{p}(\phi_{m}\phi_{m^{\prime}})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{p}{p\choose i}|\partial_{\tau}^{i}\phi_{m}||\partial_{\tau}^{p-i}\phi_{m^{\prime}}|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{p}{p\choose i}2^{i+1}c_{0}i^{3i}2^{p+1-i}c_{0}{(p-i)}^{3(p-i)}
